People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve gotten kind of stuck in these little routines. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.

It sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.

Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve gotten kind of stuck in these little routines. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.

It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go to my museum shift—there’s a lovely quiet to that routine, actually—and then this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or put someone at risk somehow.\n\nBut then it can be contamination too, like I’ve touched something unclean and missed it. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I’d say both come up. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which makes it rather irritating, but it still sticks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. Contamination is part of it, yes. I’ll have touched something and then this thought keeps nagging that it was unclean somehow. But it doesn’t stay neatly there, if that makes sense—it can turn into, “Have I put someone at risk?”

It often happens when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift. Funny, the museum has all these careful little labels and routines, very orderly, which I usually like. But then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or missed something I should have dealt with. I do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It just doesn’t stop it sticking.

And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve gotten kind of stuck in these little routines. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go to my museum shift—there’s a lovely quiet to that routine, actually—and then this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or put someone at risk somehow.\n\nBut then it can be contamination too, like I’ve touched something unclean and missed it. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I’d say both come up. I can be just reading at home—nothing dramatic, just my usual chair and a book—or getting ready for my museum shift, and then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere.\n\nBut other times it’s more that I’ve touched something unclean and haven’t dealt with it properly. And then it loops around to, well, maybe I’ve put someone at risk somehow. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which makes it rather irritating, but it still sticks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, really.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve gotten kind of stuck in these little routines. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go to my museum shift—there’s a lovely quiet to that routine, actually—and then this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or put someone at risk somehow.\n\nBut then it can be contamination too, like I’ve touched something unclean and missed it. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I’d say both come up. I can be just reading at home—nothing dramatic, just my usual chair and a book—or getting ready for my museum shift, and then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere.\n\nBut other times it’s more that I’ve touched something unclean and haven’t dealt with it properly. And then it loops around to, well, maybe I’ve put someone at risk somehow. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which makes it rather irritating, but it still sticks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, really. The harm one has a bit more bite to it, maybe. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Probably harm, in the end… though it usually starts with contamination. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift, and the thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, but then it sort of wanders into, “What if I’ve left something unsafe?” or “What if someone’s at risk because of me?”

I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. The museum is very orderly, you know, everything labelled and checked—but still, that thought just keeps pushing its way in.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve gotten kind of stuck in these little routines. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go to my museum shift—there’s a lovely quiet to that routine, actually—and then this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or put someone at risk somehow.\n\nBut then it can be contamination too, like I’ve touched something unclean and missed it. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I’d say both come up. I can be just reading at home—nothing dramatic, just my usual chair and a book—or getting ready for my museum shift, and then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere.\n\nBut other times it’s more that I’ve touched something unclean and haven’t dealt with it properly. And then it loops around to, well, maybe I’ve put someone at risk somehow. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which makes it rather irritating, but it still sticks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, really. The harm one has a bit more bite to it, maybe. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift—there’s a whole little routine to it, keys and coat and all that—and then this ugly little thought just barges in that I’ve left something unsafe.\n\nBut sometimes it’s more about having touched something unclean, and then it sort of slides into, “What if I’ve put someone at risk?” I do know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. That’s the maddening part. It still gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Contamination is part of it, yes. I’ll have touched something and then this thought keeps nagging that it was unclean somehow. But it doesn’t stay neatly there, if that makes sense—it can turn into, “Have I put someone at risk?”\n\nIt often happens when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift. Funny, the museum has all these careful little labels and routines, very orderly, which I usually like. But then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or missed something I should have dealt with. I do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It just doesn’t stop it sticking.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts mostly about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but not only contamination. It might start with, “Oh, I’ve touched something unclean,” and then—well, my mind doesn’t leave it alone. I’ll be reading at home, quite peacefully, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere, or that I’ve somehow put someone at risk.\n\nI do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It’s just… it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. It often starts as contamination—like I’ve touched something unclean—but then it turns into this worry that I’ve put someone else at risk somehow.\n\nI’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in that I’ve left something unsafe. Which is silly, I know that perfectly well—well, not silly exactly, but out of proportion. It just keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about contamination or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: Probably harm, in the end… though it usually starts with contamination. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift, and the thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, but then it sort of wanders into, “What if I’ve left something unsafe?” or “What if someone’s at risk because of me?”\n\nI know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. The museum is very orderly, you know, everything labelled and checked—but still, that thought just keeps pushing its way in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… the contamination thought often comes first, like I’ve touched something unclean. But quite quickly it turns into the harm worry — that I’ve left something unsafe or somehow put someone at risk.

It can happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift. Funny, really, the museum is all labels and careful handling, that whole calm order of things, and yet my mind goes off on this ugly little track. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, but it still barges in.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve gotten kind of stuck in these little routines. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go to my museum shift—there’s a lovely quiet to that routine, actually—and then this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or put someone at risk somehow.\n\nBut then it can be contamination too, like I’ve touched something unclean and missed it. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I’d say both come up. I can be just reading at home—nothing dramatic, just my usual chair and a book—or getting ready for my museum shift, and then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere.\n\nBut other times it’s more that I’ve touched something unclean and haven’t dealt with it properly. And then it loops around to, well, maybe I’ve put someone at risk somehow. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which makes it rather irritating, but it still sticks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, really. The harm one has a bit more bite to it, maybe. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift—there’s a whole little routine to it, keys and coat and all that—and then this ugly little thought just barges in that I’ve left something unsafe.\n\nBut sometimes it’s more about having touched something unclean, and then it sort of slides into, “What if I’ve put someone at risk?” I do know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. That’s the maddening part. It still gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Contamination is part of it, yes. I’ll have touched something and then this thought keeps nagging that it was unclean somehow. But it doesn’t stay neatly there, if that makes sense—it can turn into, “Have I put someone at risk?”\n\nIt often happens when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift. Funny, the museum has all these careful little labels and routines, very orderly, which I usually like. But then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or missed something I should have dealt with. I do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It just doesn’t stop it sticking.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts mostly about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but not only contamination. It might start with, “Oh, I’ve touched something unclean,” and then—well, my mind doesn’t leave it alone. I’ll be reading at home, quite peacefully, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere, or that I’ve somehow put someone at risk.\n\nI do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It’s just… it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. It often starts as contamination—like I’ve touched something unclean—but then it turns into this worry that I’ve put someone else at risk somehow.\n\nI’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in that I’ve left something unsafe. Which is silly, I know that perfectly well—well, not silly exactly, but out of proportion. It just keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about contamination or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: Probably harm, in the end… though it usually starts with contamination. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift, and the thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, but then it sort of wanders into, “What if I’ve left something unsafe?” or “What if someone’s at risk because of me?”\n\nI know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. The museum is very orderly, you know, everything labelled and checked—but still, that thought just keeps pushing its way in.\n\nDoctor: Is it more often the thought of contamination or the thought of causing harm that comes to your mind first?\n\nPatient: I think… the contamination thought often comes first, like I’ve touched something unclean. But quite quickly it turns into the harm worry — that I’ve left something unsafe or somehow put someone at risk.\n\nIt can happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift. Funny, really, the museum is all labels and careful handling, that whole calm order of things, and yet my mind goes off on this ugly little track. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, but it still barges in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

But then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or missed something I should have dealt with. I do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It just doesn’t stop it sticking.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts mostly about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but not only contamination. It might start with, “Oh, I’ve touched something unclean,” and then—well, my mind doesn’t leave it alone. I’ll be reading at home, quite peacefully, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere, or that I’ve somehow put someone at risk.\n\nI do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It’s just… it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. It often starts as contamination—like I’ve touched something unclean—but then it turns into this worry that I’ve put someone else at risk somehow.\n\nI’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in that I’ve left something unsafe. Which is silly, I know that perfectly well—well, not silly exactly, but out of proportion. It just keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about contamination or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: Probably harm, in the end… though it usually starts with contamination. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift, and the thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, but then it sort of wanders into, “What if I’ve left something unsafe?” or “What if someone’s at risk because of me?”\n\nI know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. The museum is very orderly, you know, everything labelled and checked—but still, that thought just keeps pushing its way in.\n\nDoctor: Is it more often the thought of contamination or the thought of causing harm that comes to your mind first?\n\nPatient: I think… the contamination thought often comes first, like I’ve touched something unclean. I’d say the harm one happens more, really — or at least it’s the one that sticks. It may start with something unclean, like I’ve touched something I shouldn’t have, but then my mind runs off with it: what if I’ve left something unsafe, what if someone’s at risk because of me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve gotten kind of stuck in these little routines. Like, I’ll handle my wrist brace and then feel like I need to wash my hands again, even if I just did. And before I leave the house I’ll recheck the stove, the front door… sometimes I’m silently counting through the steps in my head.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it out loud. I mean, I’ve always liked things orderly—I used to be pretty precise with paperwork, back when I was working—but this is different. It’s starting to make me late, even though part of me knows I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting myself ready for a shift at the museum—well, I volunteer there sometimes, mostly quiet work, which I usually like—and then this nasty little thought just sort of barges in.\n\nIt’s usually something like, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something dirty?” Or somehow I’ve put someone else at risk without realizing it. I know, logically, it’s probably out of proportion. I can tell myself that. But the thought doesn’t just leave because I’ve told it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform a specific action to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. It’s not like I have a grand reason worked out, but it feels like if I don’t do the thing—wash my hands after touching my wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again—then something might go wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll count through the steps in my head before leaving, almost like I’m making sure everything is in its proper place. I used to be very careful with paperwork, you know, back when things had to be filed just so, but this is… different. Now even when I know I’ve already checked, I still feel pulled to do it again, and then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions you're performing, like checking the stove or washing your hands, are specifically intended to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the feeling behind it, mostly. Like if I don’t wash after handling the wrist brace, or if I don’t check the stove and the front door again, then I’ve missed something important and something bad could happen.\n\nThough, it’s funny—not funny, but strange—I used to be so organized with paperwork, everything in the right folder, and that felt sensible. This doesn’t feel sensible in the same way. I’ll be standing there counting through the steps before I leave, even when I know I already did them, and then I’m late anyway. So yes, I suppose it’s meant to stop harm, but it’s gotten into these little routines I can’t quite shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about something bad happening are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… against my will is a good way to put it. I’ll be doing something perfectly ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for my museum shift—and I’m usually quite settled then, I like the quiet of it—and then this ugly little thought just barges in.\n\nIt’ll be, “What if I left something unsafe?” or “What if I touched something unclean?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. And I know, I really do know, that it’s out of proportion. But knowing that doesn’t stop it arriving. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go to my museum shift—there’s a lovely quiet to that routine, actually—and then this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or put someone at risk somehow.\n\nBut then it can be contamination too, like I’ve touched something unclean and missed it. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I’d say both come up. I can be just reading at home—nothing dramatic, just my usual chair and a book—or getting ready for my museum shift, and then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere.\n\nBut other times it’s more that I’ve touched something unclean and haven’t dealt with it properly. And then it loops around to, well, maybe I’ve put someone at risk somehow. It feels intrusive, like I didn’t invite it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions or routines you're performing need to be done in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, in a way. It’s not just doing it once—it has to feel… complete, I suppose. Like with leaving the house, I’ll silently count through the steps, stove, door, that sort of thing, and if I’m interrupted I may start over.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to keep files in order—though that was useful, really, people appreciated it—but this is different. Now I can know I’ve already washed my hands after the wrist brace, or already checked the front door, and still feel I haven’t done it the right way. Then I’m fussing with it again, and that’s when I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm to yourself or others, or about coming into contact with something contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. The harm one is there—like I’ll be reading quietly at home and suddenly think, “What if I left something unsafe?” or somehow I’ve put someone at risk. But then there’s also the unclean sort of thought, like maybe I touched something contaminated and didn’t deal with it properly.\n\nIt’s odd, because I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. I can be getting ready for my museum shift, thinking about something completely ordinary, and then that thought just barges in. Not gently, either. It sort of interrupts everything.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about causing harm or about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Probably a bit of both, though I suppose the “harm” one feels sharper. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go to my museum shift—there’s a lovely quiet to that routine, actually—and then this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or put someone at risk somehow.\n\nBut then it can be contamination too, like I’ve touched something unclean and missed it. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I’d say both come up. I can be just reading at home—nothing dramatic, just my usual chair and a book—or getting ready for my museum shift, and then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere.\n\nBut other times it’s more that I’ve touched something unclean and haven’t dealt with it properly. And then it loops around to, well, maybe I’ve put someone at risk somehow. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which makes it rather irritating, but it still sticks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, really. The harm one has a bit more bite to it, maybe. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift—there’s a whole little routine to it, keys and coat and all that—and then this ugly little thought just barges in that I’ve left something unsafe.\n\nBut sometimes it’s more about having touched something unclean, and then it sort of slides into, “What if I’ve put someone at risk?” I do know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. That’s the maddening part. It still gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Contamination is part of it, yes. I’ll have touched something and then this thought keeps nagging that it was unclean somehow. But it doesn’t stay neatly there, if that makes sense—it can turn into, “Have I put someone at risk?”\n\nIt often happens when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift. Funny, the museum has all these careful little labels and routines, very orderly, which I usually like. But then this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or missed something I should have dealt with. I do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It just doesn’t stop it sticking.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts mostly about something being contaminated?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but not only contamination. It might start with, “Oh, I’ve touched something unclean,” and then—well, my mind doesn’t leave it alone. I’ll be reading at home, quite peacefully, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe somewhere, or that I’ve somehow put someone at risk.\n\nI do know it’s out of proportion. I know that perfectly well. It’s just… it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. It often starts as contamination—like I’ve touched something unclean—but then it turns into this worry that I’ve put someone else at risk somehow.\n\nI’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for my museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in that I’ve left something unsafe. Which is silly, I know that perfectly well—well, not silly exactly, but out of proportion. It just keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about contamination or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: Probably harm, in the end… though it usually starts with contamination. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift, and the thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, but then it sort of wanders into, “What if I’ve left something unsafe?” or “What if someone’s at risk because of me?”\n\nI know perfectly well it’s out of proportion. The museum is very orderly, you know, everything labelled and checked—but still, that thought just keeps pushing its way in.\n\nDoctor: Is it more often the thought of contamination or the thought of causing harm that comes to your mind first?\n\nPatient: I think… the contamination thought often comes first, like I’ve touched something unclean. But quite quickly it turns into the harm worry — that I’ve left something unsafe or somehow put someone at risk.\n\nIt can happen when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready for a museum shift. Funny, really, the museum is all labels and careful handling, that whole calm order of things, and yet my mind goes off on this ugly little track. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, but it still barges in.\n\nDoctor: Which type of thought do you think occurs to you more frequently: thoughts about contamination or thoughts about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: I’d say the harm one happens more, really — or at least it’s the one that sticks. It may start with something unclean, like I’ve touched something I shouldn’t have, but then my mind runs off with it: what if I’ve left something unsafe, what if someone’s at risk because of me.\n\nIt’ll happen when I’m just reading at home, which is ridiculous because I’m hardly doing anything dangerous, or when I’m getting ready for my museum shift. Museums are so careful about objects and labels and all that, you’d think that would be soothing. But no, the thought barges in anyway. I do know it’s out of proportion, perfectly well. It just doesn’t seem to stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. It just arrives and gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to separate them neatly, to be honest. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which is the frustrating part. It’s not as if I really believe it, exactly. And then it loops around to, well, maybe I’ve put someone at risk somehow. I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion, which makes it rather irritating, but it still sticks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recurring thoughts are more often about something being contaminated or about causing harm to someone?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, really. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly because I’m afraid something harmful might happen if I don’t, I suppose. The washing can start with contamination — especially after handling my wrist brace, for some reason — but then it becomes, well, if I don’t wash again, have I carried something somewhere I shouldn’t have?

And with the stove and the front door it’s very much that sense of preventing something bad. I’ll recheck them, and sometimes silently count through the steps before I leave. It’s embarrassing, really, because I know I’ve already done enough, but I get stuck in it and then I’m late.
